In the past 30 days there have been three confirmed detections in dairy cows, with Texas reporting two affected herds and Utah reporting a single detection. So far this year 85 cattle herds have had confirmed H5N1 infections. Last year APHIS tracked 171 H5N1 outbreaks in cattle. In 2024, the year the virus was first detected in cows, the United States saw 917 herds infected. Poultry operation hit South Dakota is reporting a large H5N1 outbreak among commercial poultry breeding operations in Edmunds County, according to a separate APHIS tracker, with 29,700 birds affected. South Dakota reported another outbreak in Brule County with 2,800 birds. In Idaho, two smaller outbreaks were confirmed in birds in Ada and Canyon counties. In the past 30 days, APHIS has confirmed outbreaks in two commercial and three backyard US flocks. CDC, FDA end investigation into Listeria outbreak, begin looking into new Salmonella cluster News brief Mary Van Beusekom, MS Topics Foodborne Disease Listeria Salmonella Cyclospora Marta Miranda / Flickr cc As the US Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) and the Food and Drug Administration (FDA) end their investigation into the June Listeria outbreak tied to soft cheese, the FDA is reporting a new outbreak, this one involving Salmonella from an unidentified food source. The CDC has also released updated case counts for several smaller Cyclospora outbreaks. New 84-case Salmonella Newport outbreak Yesterday, the two agencies declared the four-state Listeria monocytogenes outbreak traced to soft cheese over after reporting three new cases and four new hospitalizations, for a total of 15 and 14, respectively. One person died. The cheese manufacturer, Maryland-based Clover Hill Dairy, recalled its ricotta-like requeson cheese and all other cheese products in June. Also yesterday, the FDA reported a new 84-case Salmonella Newport outbreak tied to a yet-to-be-identified food source. No other details have been released. The FDA updated case counts for three Cyclospora outbreaks with unknown food sources, adding five, two, and one cases, for totals of 22, 203, and 28, respectively. The case total for all US summer Cyclospora outbreaks, including the one linked to contaminated iceberg lettuce, is now 17,180 after the CDC added 3,285 infections this week. The CDC also said it is investigating another 11,844 cases that are not yet confirmed. At least two people have died of cyclosporiasis this summer, and 922 have been hospitalized. Improved hand hygiene had no impact on detection of multidrug-resistant organisms, hospital study finds News brief Chris Dall, MA Topics Healthcare-Associated Infections Yoo Chung / Flickr cc A study conducted at four hospitals in Atlanta found that improved hand hygiene had no impact on detection of multidrug-resistant organisms (MDROs), researchers reported today in Infection Control & Hospital Epidemiology. For the study, a team led by researchers at Emory University School of Medicine analyzed correlations between monthly unit-level hand hygiene adherence and rates of hospital-onset MDROs and other pathogens of epidemiologic significance (MDRO+) that can cause healthcare-associated infections (HAIs). Hand hygiene adherence was determined by an electronic monitoring system in patients’ rooms that records whether a healthcare worker dispensed hand hygiene product after entering the room. The study authors note that while it’s widely accepted that high hand hygiene adherence reduces HAI incidence, many of the studies looking at the relationship between the two often rely on direct observation of hand hygiene adherence, which can be prone to overestimates. “The limitations of direct observation have generated interest in alternative methods for hand hygiene monitoring,” they wrote. “Automated hand hygiene monitoring systems (AHHMS) are an appealing solution to overcome the pitfalls of direct observation.” Complex interplay between hand hygiene, MDRO acquisition More than 22.7 million hand hygiene opportunities were recorded by the AHHMS during the study period: January 2021 through September 2022. The mean monthly hand hygiene adherence rate increased from 41% in January 2021 to 57% in September 2022 on average across all units and facilities. A total of 1,847 MDRO+ detections were observed in 752,341 patient days, with a median composite MDRO+ rate of 1.7 per 1,000 patient-days. Despite the hand hygiene improvement, the researchers were unable to demonstrate a clear unit-level inverse association between hand hygiene adherence and MDRO+ detection (rate ratio, 1.01). They say the results like reflect the “complex interaction between the two variable and unmeasured confounders.” “The interplay between hand hygiene, infection prevention efforts, and MDRO acquisition is complex, and an absence of proof does not negate the importance of hand hygiene,” they wrote. Time
The US Department of Agriculture’s Animal and Plant Health Inspection Service (APHIS) confirmed new H5N1 avian flu cases in the past 30 days, with updates reported on 2026-08-27.
Location and population
The outbreaks were confirmed in commercial poultry and dairy cattle in South Dakota, Texas, and Utah, with specific mention of South Dakota's Edmunds County and Brule County, and Texas and Utah's dairy herds.
Event
USDA confirmed new H5N1 avian flu cases in poultry and dairy cattle, with three confirmed detections in dairy cows and a large outbreak in South Dakota's poultry breeding operations.
Scale, impact and risk
So far this year, 85 cattle herds have had confirmed H5N1 infections. In South Dakota, 29,700 birds were affected in one outbreak, and 2,800 in another. Last year, APHIS tracked 171 H5N1 outbreaks in cattle, and in 2024, 917 herds were infected.
Response, status and uncertainty
The USDA is actively tracking and confirming H5N1 outbreaks in cattle and poultry. No further details on specific measures or risk assessments were provided.

Bird flu is spreading. Your backyard feeder could make it worse - Science Daily

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Bird flu is spreading. Your backyard feeder could make it worse - Science Daily

News Brief
The article discusses the potential role of backyard bird feeders in exacerbating the spread of H5N1 in Australia. It highlights that as the bird flu outbreak spreads, these feeders may act as unexpected hotspots by bringing bird species together in unnatural ways. Experts suggest that stopping feeding is the safest approach, though smaller amounts of food may be acceptable. The article does not provide specific case counts, death counts, or detailed risk assessments related to H5N1. The findings are based on observations and expert recommendations rather than confirmed data. The study emphasizes the need for caution in managing bird feeders to mitigate the risk of disease transmission among avian populations.

H5N1 bird flu: what you need to know - Australian Conservation Foundation
新闻简报澳大利亚保护基金会 (ACF) 题为“H5N1 禽流感:您需要了解的信息”的文章提供了有关 H5N1 禽流感的一般信息,强调其对野生动物的影响以及公众认识的必要性。该报告于 2026 年 8 月 24 日发布,没有报告已确认的 暴发 或具体事件,但强调该病毒对生态系统和人类健康的担忧。ACF 鼓励读者采取行动,例如签署请愿书或捐款,以支持保护自然的努力。不过,文章没有提供详细的病例数、死亡数或暴露数量,也没有提及与 H5N1 禽流感相关的任何具体调查、测试或遏制措施。相反,它侧重于宣传和公众参与,敦促个人为保护工作做出贡献。内容是完整原始报告的一部分,不包含由于联合摘要而造成的任何限制。

H5N1 bird flu: what you need to know - Australian Conservation Foundation

News Brief
The article titled 'H5N1 bird flu: what you need to know' by the Australian Conservation Foundation (ACF) provides general information about H5N1 bird flu, emphasizing its impact on wildlife and the need for public awareness. Published on August 24, 2026, it does not report a confirmed outbreak or specific event but highlights the virus as a concern for ecosystems and human health. The ACF encourages readers to take action, such as signing petitions or donating, to support efforts in protecting nature. However, the article does not provide detailed case counts, death counts, or exposure numbers, nor does it mention any specific investigation, testing, or containment measures related to H5N1 bird flu. Instead, it focuses on advocacy and public engagement, urging individuals to contribute to conservation efforts. The content is part of a full original report and does not include any limitations due to syndicated summaries.

B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

澳大利亚确认首例哺乳动物 H5N1 禽流感病例

Australia confirms first H5N1 bird flu case in mammal - سانا
新闻简报继在南澳大利亚海滩港发现的一只死亡的长鼻海豹体内检测到病毒后,澳大利亚确认了首例哺乳动物感染 H5N1 禽流感病例。澳大利亚新闻社报道了这一事件,并得到了联邦农业部长朱莉·柯林斯的证实。这只海豹被发现时没有明显的损伤,它的死亡与其他海洋哺乳动物的疾病或死亡无关。该病毒今年通过候鸟到达澳大利亚,促使野生动物加强监测,以防止其可能传播到其他物种。当局已确认数百起鸟类病例,但尚未在家禽或牲畜中发现感染病例。澳大利亚卫生和兽医当局表示,对人类健康的风险仍然很低。此病例发生之际,野生鸟类 中的 H5N1 感染不断增加,南澳大利亚出现大规模海鸟死亡。目前情况正在调查中,尚未报道有关传播或影响的进一步细节。
查看新闻五要素
时间
2026 年 8 月 23 日,澳大利亚确认首例哺乳动物 H5N1 禽流感病例。该病毒是在南澳大利亚海滩港发现的一只死亡的长鼻海豹身上检测到的。该病例发生之际,野生鸟类 中的 H5N1 感染不断增加,南澳大利亚今年出现了大规模海鸟死亡事件。
地点与对象
该病例在南澳大利亚海滩港得到证实。受影响的种群包括野生鸟类和海洋哺乳动物,长鼻海狗是第一个报告感染H5N1禽流感的哺乳动物。
事件
澳大利亚新闻社报道了这一事件,并得到了联邦农业部长朱莉·柯林斯的证实。
规模、影响与风险
当局已确认数百起鸟类病例,但尚未在家禽或牲畜中发现感染病例。澳大利亚卫生和兽医当局表示,对人类健康的风险仍然很低。该病毒今年通过候鸟到达澳大利亚,促使野生动物加强监测,以防止其可能传播到其他物种。
应对、状态与不确定性
通过对长鼻海狗的检测证实了这一病例。当局正在监测病毒在自然环境中的传播,并加强了对野生动物的监测。据称对人类健康的风险较低,在家禽或牲畜中未发现感染。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Australia confirms first H5N1 bird flu case in mammal - سانا

News Brief
Australia has confirmed its first case of H5N1 bird flu in a mammal, following the detection of the virus in a long-nosed fur seal found dead in Beachport, South Australia. The event was reported by the Australian News Agency and confirmed by Federal Agriculture Minister Julie Collins. The seal was discovered without visible injuries, and its death was not linked to illness or deaths among other marine mammals. The virus reached Australia this year through migratory birds, prompting increased wildlife monitoring for its possible spread to other species. Authorities have confirmed hundreds of cases among birds, but no infections have been detected in poultry or livestock. The risk to human health remains low, according to Australian health and veterinary authorities. This case comes amid rising H5N1 infections among wild birds, with mass seabird deaths recorded in South Australia. The situation is under investigation, and no further details on the spread or impact have been reported.

B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

“毁灭性”:禽流感威胁旗舰物种,西澳大利亚州担心黑天鹅时刻 - 《卫报》

‘Devastating’: Western Australia fears a black swan moment as bird flu threatens flagship species - The Guardian
新闻简报Western Australia is facing a potential 'black swan moment' as bird flu, specifically the H5N1 variant, threatens its flagship species, the black swan. The black swan, which serves as the state's bird emblem, is considered an indicator species for H5N1 due to its high visibility and low survival rates when infected. Experts warn of 'significant mortality' if the virus spreads to black swans, as they are ranked as 'very high risk' of infection. The WA government is testing a vaccine in five endangered black cockatoos at Perth zoo, produced by Zoetis, originally designed for chickens. However, the vaccine is not registered for use in wildlife and requires assessment before being used on native birds. The government has spent $113m on bird flu preparedness, but the effectiveness of the vaccine in native species remains uncertain. The virus has already caused three mass mortality events in South Australia involving over 1,000 greater crested terns, highlighting the potential for widespread impact on native bird populations. The black swan holds cultural and totemic significance for the Noongar people, adding to the emotional and ecological stakes of the situation.

时间

地点与对象
西澳大利亚,黑天鹅(H5N1 指示物种,种群可见度高,存活率低)。
事件
由于禽流感威胁到旗舰物种,西澳大利亚州担心出现黑天鹅时刻;黑天鹅被列为感染 H5N1 病毒的“极高风险”,专家警告说,如果病毒传播给黑天鹅,它们将面临“严重的 死亡率”。该州的鸟徽是 H5N1 的指示物种,种群可见度高,存活率低。西澳政府正在珀斯动物园的五只濒临灭绝的黑凤头鹦鹉身上测试 疫苗。
规模、影响与风险
该病毒已在南澳大利亚引发了三起大规模 死亡率 事件,涉及 1,000 多只大凤头燕鸥。专家警告说,受感染的鸟类可能会导致整个群体灭绝,并且失去整个种群的可能性很大。该物种被列为感染该病毒的“极高风险”。
应对、状态与不确定性
The vaccine is produced by Zoetis and originally designed for chickens, but it is not registered for use in wildlife. The feasibility of vaccination in black swans is being explored, and the government has spent $113m on bird flu preparedness. However, the strength of evidence that the vaccine will work in native species is not disclosed.
B routine priorityPublic-health news

‘Devastating’: Western Australia fears a black swan moment as bird flu threatens flagship species - The Guardian

News Brief
Western Australia is facing a potential 'black swan moment' as bird flu, specifically the H5N1 variant, threatens its flagship species, the black swan. The black swan, which serves as the state's bird emblem, is considered an indicator species for H5N1 due to its high visibility and low survival rates when infected. Experts warn of 'significant mortality' if the virus spreads to black swans, as they are ranked as 'very high risk' of infection. The WA government is testing a vaccine in five endangered black cockatoos at Perth zoo, produced by Zoetis, originally designed for chickens. However, the vaccine is not registered for use in wildlife and requires assessment before being used on native birds. The government has spent $113m on bird flu preparedness, but the effectiveness of the vaccine in native species remains uncertain. The virus has already caused three mass mortality events in South Australia involving over 1,000 greater crested terns, highlighting the potential for widespread impact on native bird populations. The black swan holds cultural and totemic significance for the Noongar people, adding to the emotional and ecological stakes of the situation.

Skip to main contentSkip to navigation Close dialogue1/2Next imagePrevious imageToggle caption Support the Guardian Fund independent journalism with $15 per month Support us Support us Print subscriptions Newsletters Sign in US US edition UK edition Australia edition Europe edition International edition The Guardian - Back to homeThe Guardian News Opinion Sport Culture Lifestyle Show moreHide expanded menu News View all News US news US politics Midterms 2026 World news Climate crisis Middle East Ukraine US immigration Soccer Business Environment Tech Science Newsletters The Filter Wellness Opinion View all Opinion The Guardian view Columnists Letters Opinion videos Cartoons Sport View all Sport Soccer NFL Tennis MLB MLS NBA WNBA NHL F1 Golf Culture View all Culture Film Books Music Art & design TV & radio Stage Classical Games Design a better world Lifestyle View all Lifestyle The Filter Wellness Fashion Food Recipes Love & sex Home & garden Health & fitness Family Travel Money Search input google-search Search Support us Print subscriptions Newsletters Download the app Search jobs Digital Archive Guardian Licensing Live events About Us The Guardian app Video Podcasts Pictures Inside the Guardian Guardian Weekly Crosswords Wordiply Corrections Tips Search input google-search Search Search jobs Digital Archive Guardian Licensing Live events About Us Environment Climate crisis Wildlife Energy Pollution Green light The black swan, which has come to define the west, has been ranked as ‘very high risk’ of becoming infected with the bird flu virus. Photograph: Christopher Hopkins/AAP View image in fullscreen The black swan, which has come to define the west, has been ranked as ‘very high risk’ of becoming infected with the bird flu virus. Photograph: Christopher Hopkins/AAP ‘Devastating’: Western Australia fears a black swan moment as bird flu threatens flagship species The state’s bird emblem is an indicator species for H5N1, with high visibility populations and low survival rates. Experts are warning of ‘significant mortality’ Follow our Australia news live blog for latest updates Get our breaking news email, free app or daily news podcast When the black swans start dying, Western Australians will know that bird flu has truly arrived. The birds are an “indicator species” because once they are infected with the H5N1 virus, they are more likely to die than survive, according to the University of Queensland virologist Kirsty Short. “If avian influenza gets into a place where there are black swans ... then I think we will see swan mortality and if there’s a significant number of swans, significant mortality.” More than 1,000 birds found dead in three mass mortality events amid ‘significant’ H5 escalation in Australia Read more WA’s black swans are deeply woven into its identity. As the state’s official bird emblem, the species is emblazoned on buildings, stamps, the flag and the coat of arms. Now the bird that has come to define the west is facing devastating consequences. Unlike many birds, black swans lack a key immune protein involved in recognising viral infections, a difference that may leave them vulnerable to a killer inflammatory response. The species has been ranked as “very high risk” of becoming infected with the virus, according to a commonwealth national risk assessment. Experts don’t yet know how black swans will respond to living with a virus that, once established, is expected to circulate and evolve in wild bird populations. ‘Potential’ to lose entire populations Black swans live in both coastal and inland freshwater areas and can form large colonies when breeding. An infected bird could cause an entire colony to die out, the ornithologist Floyd Holmes from Phoenix Environmental Sciences says. “It is not likely to wipe out all of the swans, but for any local groups of swans, particularly in the south-west, that all congregate and breed in a single place, the potential to just lose that entire population all at once is definitely there,” Holmes says. “It would be pretty devastating for that local area.” Sign up for the Breaking News Australia email South Australia, where the virus was first reported, has already reported three mass mortality events involving more than 1,000 greater crested terns. Holmes says the public is also more likely to notice the impacts on black swans because the birds are so large and distinctive. Nine weeks after a migratory bird brought H5N1 to WA shores – and five years after this variant of the virus reached North America – the WA government says it is now testing the vaccine in five endangered black cockatoos at Perth zoo. “The feasibility of vaccination in black swans is being explored,” the WA environment minister, Matthew Swinbourn, says. “This is just one tool in the fight against the disease.” View image in fullscreen For the Noongar people, the black swan is called Maarli or Kuljak and carries a totemic significance that predates its adoption as a state symbol. Photograph: Benny Marty/Alamy The federal agriculture minister, Julie Collins, says the government has spent $113m on bird flu preparedness, including purchasing $2m worth of vaccine to protect captive populations of priority species. The vaccine is produced by US animal health company Zoetis and was originally designed for use in chickens. It is not registered for use in wildlife and would need to be assessed before its use can be extended to native birds, Swinbourn says. The government was tight-lipped about the strength of evidence that the vaccine will work in native species. “The vaccination rollout led by states and territories has commenced and will continue across the country in the coming weeks and months,” a Department of Agriculture, Fisheries and Forestry spokesperson says. skip past newsletter promotion after newsletter promotion Birds require two doses of the vaccine, weeks apart, so it is not feasible to vaccinate most wild populations, experts say. Efforts have begun to vaccinate 5,000 little penguins in wild colonies in Victoria, a process only considered possible because the colonies are well-studied, used to humans and return to the same burrows every night. Short says until now, there has been a lack of investment in research to understand what avian influenza and avian vaccines do in native species. “There were some studies that were done vaccinating different birds in a zoo with a H5N1 vaccine, and they found that parrots didn’t mount a very good antibody response for some reason,” Short says. “So there are obviously species-dependent variations, and I think we really should have invested in understanding what this virus means specifically for Australia.” Birds already under threat The BirdLife Australia waterbird ecologist Lindall Kidd says WA is now playing catch up after much of its bird flu preparedness funding was directed towards agriculture. “Our birds are already under pressure from habitat loss through land clearing and climate change, we could be looking at death by a thousand cuts,” she says. Bird flu has devastated wildlife around the world. What Australian birds are most at risk? Read more “There was a known gap in preparedness prior to the bird flu incursion, and we did not have enough plans in place especially for some of our more common, widely spread species. They are the ones in our urban areas, like pelicans and swans,” Kidd says. She says the psychosocial effect of losing large numbers of swans will be “massive”. For the Noongar people of south-west WA, the black swan is called Maarli or Kuljak and it carries a totemic significance that predates its adoption as a state symbol, according to Murdoch University Noongar researcher Glen Stasiuk. “With connections to brotherhood and the seasons, it is a very important bird,” Stasiuk says. While the future of WA’s bird emblem remains unknown, Holmes puts the possibilities simply. “Option one is they develop an immunity to the virus, option two is that the virus doesn’t end up reaching all of them, or option three is we lose them all,” Holmes says. “I suspect that the number of swans is going to go down. Hopefully it doesn’t reach zero. I don’t think it will, but I guess time will tell.” Explore more on these topics Western Australia Bird flu Birds Wildlife Animals features Share Reuse this content Most viewed Senator calls for criminal investigation of RFK Jr after Guardian report Dolly Parton obituary Warning of fresh danger from rising lake water in Nepal – as it happened Ukraine war briefing: Missiles shot down during long Russian attack wave Jon Ossoff went low on Natalie Harp. Location and population
Western Australia, black swans (indicator species for H5N1, with high visibility populations and low survival rates).
Event
Western Australia fears a black swan moment as bird flu threatens flagship species; black swans are ranked as 'very high risk' of becoming infected with the H5N1 virus, and experts warn of 'significant mortality' if the virus spreads to them. The state’s bird emblem is an indicator species for H5N1, with high visibility populations and low survival rates. The WA government is testing a vaccine in five endangered black cockatoos at Perth zoo.
Scale, impact and risk
The virus has already caused three mass mortality events in South Australia involving over 1,000 greater crested terns. Experts warn that an infected bird could cause an entire colony to die out, and the potential to lose entire populations is significant. The species has been ranked as 'very high risk' of becoming infected with the virus.
Response, status and uncertainty
The vaccine is produced by Zoetis and originally designed for chickens, but it is not registered for use in wildlife. The feasibility of vaccination in black swans is being explored, and the government has spent $113m on bird flu preparedness. However, the strength of evidence that the vaccine will work in native species is not disclosed.
原文 / Source

B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

Alveo Technologies 与 AgriNerds 合作,为美国家禽养殖户打造首个动态 禽流感 监测 系统 - 美通社

Alveo Technologies Partners with AgriNerds to Create First-of-its-Kind, Dynamic Avian Influenza Surveillance System for U.S. Poultry Farmers - PR Newswire
新闻简报Alveo Technologies 和 AgriNerds 合作为美国家禽养殖户创建了动态 禽流感 监测 系统。该系统将快速分子测试与基于候鸟数据的 高致病性禽流感 (HPAI) 实时建模相结合。该计划旨在在一小时内为农民提供精确的结果,比传统的 PCR 检测(可能需要数天)要快得多。该平台利用机器学习自动解释测试结果,提高风险管理效率。此次合作旨在支持农场运行主动 监测 计划,减少 HPAI 暴发 的影响。该系统目前正在实施,旨在提高家禽养殖户及时应对潜在威胁的能力。
查看新闻五要素
时间
正在搜索您的内容。
地点与对象
请更改您的搜索词并重试。
事件
Alveo Technologies 和 AgriNerds 为美国家禽养殖户开发了动态 禽流感 监测 系统,该系统将快速分子测试与候鸟的实时 HPAI 建模相结合。
规模、影响与风险
该系统旨在为无症状 监测 检测提供精确、快速的结果,将传统 PCR 结果所需的时间从几天缩短到一个小时以内。它旨在帮助农民管理农场层面的风险。
应对、状态与不确定性
该系统正在开发和实施中,目标是实现动态的、风险告知的 监测 计划。它结合了机器学习来自动解释测试结果。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Alveo Technologies Partners with AgriNerds to Create First-of-its-Kind, Dynamic Avian Influenza Surveillance System for U.S. Poultry Farmers - PR Newswire

News Brief
Alveo Technologies and AgriNerds have partnered to create a dynamic avian influenza surveillance system for U.S. poultry farmers. This system combines rapid molecular testing with real-time modeling of highly pathogenic avian influenza (HPAI) based on migratory bird data. The initiative aims to provide farmers with precise results within an hour, significantly faster than traditional PCR testing, which can take days. The platform uses machine learning to interpret test results automatically, enhancing efficiency in risk management. This collaboration is designed to support farms in running proactive surveillance programs, reducing the impact of HPAI outbreaks. The system is currently being implemented and is intended to improve the ability of poultry farmers to respond to potential threats in a timely manner.
Original source text (English)

Inquiries Editorial Bureaus Partnerships Media Inquiries Worldwide Offices Hamburger menu Send a Release ALL CONTACT INFO Contact Us 888-776-0942 from 8 AM - 10 PM ET Alveo Technologies Partners with AgriNerds to Create First-of-its-Kind, Dynamic Avian Influenza Surveillance System for U.S. Poultry Farmers News provided by Alveo Technologies Aug 25, 2026, 09:02 ET Share this article Share toX Share this article Share toX Linking on-site, rapid molecular HPAI testing to real-time HPAI modeling of migratory birds to help farmers run dynamic, risk-informed surveillance programs ALAMEDA, Calif., Aug. 25, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- Alveo Technologies, Inc., a global leader in rapid, point-of-need molecular diagnostics for early disease detection, today announced a strategic partnership with AgriNerds Inc, which has developed the Waterfowl Alert Network, a first-of-its-kind dynamic surveillance and awareness program that predicts historic and daily waterfowl activity around the nation's poultry and livestock farms. The partnership will pair Alveo's unique, rapid, molecular diagnostic with AgriNerds' Waterfowl Alert Network, a proprietary system that uses a combination of satellite imagery, weather radar, and telemetry to create daily farm-level HPAI risk scores based on remote sensing and HPAI surveillance data. To defend against HPAI and other pathogens, poultry producers have heavily invested in strengthening their biosecurity programs, but farms continue to suffer from HPAI outbreaks triggered by migratory birds, which is the source of infection for 68% of US H5N1 outbreaks since 2022, according to the APHIS. Currently, producers typically take one of two strategies for testing. Most test only when animals display flu-like symptoms, but this approach risks detecting the virus too late to prevent it from spreading. Some conduct periodic blind surveillance testing, which has the advantage of enabling early detection, but can be expensive. Alveo and AgriNerds are partnering to develop a new rapid and targeted approach: dynamic surveillance that alerts farmers when the risk of infection is high. AgriNerds' Waterfowl Alert Network will use radar imagery to detect migratory bird movements, analyze the data using AI-powered pattern recognition software, and generate daily risk scores it shares with customers. When risk escalates, Alveo's on-farm molecular test will help farms to conduct asymptomatic surveillance testing that provides precise results in under an hour, as opposed to waiting days for traditional PCR results to arrive from a lab. This targeted, risk-based testing approach is designed to lower costs and increase effectiveness, so farmers can detect the virus early and reduce its spread. "To stop the spread of HPAI, producers need tools that will enable them to monitor risk and, when it's elevated, employ rapid, accurate tests so they can take quick action and prevent further infection," said Shaun Holt, CEO of Alveo Technologies "This dynamic solution will aggregate and simplify rich data from AgriNerds' and deliver sophisticated risk assessments to users phones in a simple red, yellow and green format. They can then run surveillance tests based on informed risk and rapidly respond if bird flu is detected." The two organizations will use this feedback loop to strengthen the accuracy of risk assessments over time. As positive and negative test data accumulate across regions and seasons, both the risk alerts and testing guidance will become increasingly precise and valuable, securing the nation's food supply as well as producers' businesses. "Biosecurity extends far beyond the facility's fence line," said Maurice Pitesky, professor at UC Davis and CEO and co-founder of AgriNerds. "Our Waterfowl Alert Network gives farmers visibility into the dynamic risk landscape around their operations. When paired with Alveo's testing capability and rapid results, farmers can respond to real risks in real time. In this way, agriculture can move past reactive disease management to adopt a proactive, risk-informed approach." About AgriNerds: AgriNerds' veterinarians, epidemiologists, engineers, and waterfowl biologists are leveraging and repurposing various remote sensing-based technologies and applying modern statistical methods to help farmers usher in practical "off-site" risk assessments and HPAI risk predictions. The WaterFowl Alert Network is used by over 1,500 farms across 25 states and has the capacity to make historic and daily risk predictions for the entire U.S. poultry and livestock industry at the farm level. For more information, visit www.agrinerds.com. About Alveo Technologies: Alveo Technologies is a molecular diagnostics company that has developed a portable, cloud-connected platform that brings laboratory-quality pathogen detection directly to the Point of NeedTM for farmers, veterinarians and public health officials. By decentralizing molecular diagnostics, Alveo enables rapid detection and decision-making to help protect animal and human health, food security and supply chain resiliency. Alveo Sense Poultry provides avian influenza detection in approximately one hour, with results delivered through the Alveo Sense app. The Alveo platform incorporates machine learning technology to analyze assay data and provide automated interpretation of test results. Its growing portfolio includes molecular tests for avian influenza and other high-consequence pathogens. To learn more, visit alveotechnologies.com or follow us on LinkedIn. Product Availability Disclaimer: Product availability may vary by country or jurisdiction and is subject to applicable regulatory requirements and approvals. For information regarding availability in your country or jurisdiction, please contact Alveo Technologies at support@alveotechnologies.com. B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

因禽流感风险,澳大利亚将从亚南极岛屿撤离科学家

Australia to evacuate scientists from sub-Antarctic island over bird flu risk
新闻简报Australia has announced plans to evacuate scientists and staff from its research station on Macquarie Island, a remote sub-Antarctic territory, due to the "psychosocial risk" of a potential H5N1 avian influenza outbreak. The Department of Climate Change, Energy, the Environment and Water (DCCEEW) stated that while the highly pathogenic H5N1 strain has not been detected on the island, experts predict a high likelihood of significant mortality events during the upcoming breeding season. The island, home to up to 100,000 seals and millions of seabirds, including over 850,000 breeding pairs of royal penguins, is a key site for scientific research. The evacuation is scheduled for late September or early October 2026, with the RSV Nuyina set to transport the staff. The DCCEEW emphasized that the risk to humans is considered low, but the psychosocial impact of living near potentially affected animals is a major concern. The government remains committed to maintaining a long-term presence on the island, with monitoring options under consideration.
查看新闻五要素
时间
The Australian government announced the evacuation plan on Friday, 2026-08-28. The RSV Nuyina is scheduled to travel to the island in late September or early October 2026.
地点与对象
麦夸里岛是澳大利亚的一个偏远领地,位于澳大利亚大陆东南约2,000公里处。该岛栖息着多达100,000只海豹和数百万只海鸟,其中包括超过850,000对特有的王企鹅繁殖对。
事件
由于潜在的 H5N1 禽流感 暴发 的“心理社会风险”,澳大利亚政府将暂时从位于麦格理岛的澳大利亚南极计划研究站撤回工作人员。该事件已得到 DCCEEW 的确认。
规模、影响与风险
岛上尚未发现高致病性 H5N1 禽流感病毒株,但专家预测,在即将到来的繁殖季节,极有可能发生重大 死亡率 事件。对人类的风险被认为很低,但生活在潜在大量受影响动物附近的心理社会风险值得关注。
应对、状态与不确定性
DCCEEW 正在考虑监测岛上局势的方案。澳大利亚南极计划的旗舰研究和补给船 RSV Nuyina 将于 2026 年 9 月下旬或 10 月初前往该岛取回工作人员和重要货物。应对措施正在调查和规划中。

Australia to evacuate scientists from sub-Antarctic island over bird flu risk

News Brief
Australia has announced plans to evacuate scientists and staff from its research station on Macquarie Island, a remote sub-Antarctic territory, due to the "psychosocial risk" of a potential H5N1 avian influenza outbreak. The Department of Climate Change, Energy, the Environment and Water (DCCEEW) stated that while the highly pathogenic H5N1 strain has not been detected on the island, experts predict a high likelihood of significant mortality events during the upcoming breeding season. The island, home to up to 100,000 seals and millions of seabirds, including over 850,000 breeding pairs of royal penguins, is a key site for scientific research. The evacuation is scheduled for late September or early October 2026, with the RSV Nuyina set to transport the staff. The DCCEEW emphasized that the risk to humans is considered low, but the psychosocial impact of living near potentially affected animals is a major concern. The government remains committed to maintaining a long-term presence on the island, with monitoring options under consideration.
Original source text (English)
Australia to evacuate scientists from sub-Antarctic island over bird flu risk-Xinhua Australia to evacuate scientists from sub-Antarctic island over bird flu risk Source: Xinhua Editor: huaxia 2026-08-28 10:59:45 CANBERRA, Aug. 28 (Xinhua) -- The Australian government said on Friday it will evacuate scientists from a sub-Antarctic island due to the "psychosocial risk" of a potential H5N1 avian influenza outbreak. The Department of Climate Change, Energy, the Environment and Water (DCCEEW) said it would temporarily withdraw staff from the Australian Antarctic Program's research station on Macquarie Island, a remote Australian territory located about 2,000 km southeast of the mainland. The DCCEEW said that the highly pathogenic H5N1 strain of bird flu has not been detected on the island, but that experts predict a high likelihood of significant mortality events during the upcoming breeding season. The island, which was added to UNESCO's World Heritage List for its outstanding universal value in 1997, is inhabited by up to 100,000 seals and millions of seabirds, including over 850,000 breeding pairs of endemic royal penguins, at various times throughout the year. "This is an unusual measure, but it's the most appropriate course of action based on our assessments of risk and operational realities," DCCEEW Deputy Secretary Sean Sullivan said in a statement. "While the risk to humans of H5 bird flu is considered low, we can't ignore the psychosocial risks of living near potentially large numbers of affected animals." The department said that the Australian Antarctic Program's flagship research and supply vessel, the RSV Nuyina, will travel to the island in late September or early October to retrieve 24 scientists, tradespeople and support staff as well as essential cargo. Options to monitor the situation on the island are under consideration and Sullivan said Australia remains committed to maintaining a long-term presence on Macquarie Island. ■ Australia to evacuate scientists from sub-Antarctic island over bird flu risk

The response is under investigation and planning.

澳大利亚大陆首例哺乳动物死亡后,后院喂鸟器导致 H5N1 病毒传播 - 科技时报

Backyard Bird Feeders Drive H5N1 Spread After First Australian Mainland Mammal Death - Tech Times
新闻简报2026 年 8 月 23 日,澳大利亚确认首例大陆哺乳动物死于 H5N1 禽流感,此前南澳大利亚海滩港附近一只长鼻海豹检测结果呈阳性。澳大利亚联邦科学与工业研究组织 (CSIRO) 证实,该病例是在全国范围内检测到 H5N1 病毒迅速升级后发生的,其中包括 297 例确诊病例以及大凤头燕鸥和小企鹅等海鸟的大规模死亡。研究人员警告说,后院喂鸟器将多种鸟类集中在不自然的高密度下,无意中形成了 传播 热点,加速了病毒传播到新物种和环境中。虽然人类的风险仍然很低,没有本地感染的报道,但哺乳动物(包括负鼠、狐狸和塔斯马尼亚袋獾)通过清除受感染的尸体而面临更高的暴露风险。当局敦促公众停止或改变鸟类喂养方式,理由是有证据表明 野生鸟类 的饮食中不到 10% 来自喂食器,从而最大限度地减少对鸟类种群的伤害。暴发 还威胁着橙腹鹦鹉等极度濒危物种,增加了灭绝的风险。
查看新闻五要素
时间
活动日期:2026年8月19日(海狗发现);发布日期:2026年8月25日。时间范围:2024年春季至2026年8月。
地点与对象
澳大利亚:南澳大利亚州海滩港(阿德莱德以南约 310 公里的沿海城镇);西澳大利亚州勒格兰德角国家公园;南澳大利亚州和塔斯马尼亚州;全国范围。
事件
南澳大利亚州首席兽医官兼联邦农业部长于 2026 年 8 月 23 日报告,首次确认澳大利亚大陆哺乳动物因长鼻海狗 H5N1 禽流感 死亡。该事件已通过 CSIRO 测试得到证实。同时发布的指南警告说,后院喂鸟器可能会通过增加跨物种接触而推动 H5N1 病毒传播。
规模、影响与风险
全国:截至2026年8月22日,确诊H5N1病例297例;> 1,000 只大凤头燕鸥和 > 24 只小企鹅被杀死;8月初银鸥呈阳性。风险:支线热点可能加速向农业区和城市地区蔓延;哺乳动物(例如负鼠、狐狸、塔斯马尼亚袋獾、袋鼬、袋鼠、小袋鼠)通过清除受感染的鸟类尸体而面临暴露。人类风险仍然很低;澳大利亚未发现人类感染。
应对、状态与不确定性
CSIRO 的澳大利亚疾病预防中心确认了 诊断。当局承认,一旦 H5N1 在环境中传播,就无法避免重大损失。尚无更广泛的海洋哺乳动物 暴发 的证据。敦促公众停止或改变鸟类喂养方式;研究表明,鸟类从喂食器获得的饮食不足 10%,从而减轻了停止喂食造成的伤害。建议野生动物护理人员和兽医遵守生物安全规程。橙腹鹦鹉等极度濒危物种面临灭绝风险。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Backyard Bird Feeders Drive H5N1 Spread After First Australian Mainland Mammal Death - Tech Times

News Brief
Australia confirmed its first mainland mammal death from H5N1 avian influenza on August 23, 2026, after a long-nosed fur seal tested positive near Beachport, South Australia. The case, verified by CSIRO, follows a rapid escalation of H5N1 detections nationwide, including 297 confirmed cases and mass mortalities among seabirds such as greater crested terns and little penguins. Researchers warn that backyard bird feeders are inadvertently creating transmission hotspots by concentrating multiple bird species at unnaturally high densities, accelerating viral spread into new species and environments. While human risk remains low with no local infections reported, mammals—including possums, foxes, and Tasmanian devils—face elevated exposure through scavenging infected carcasses. Authorities urge the public to cease or modify bird feeding, citing evidence that wild birds derive less than 10% of their diet from feeders, minimizing harm to avian populations. The outbreak also threatens critically endangered species like the orange-bellied parrot, raising extinction risks.
Original source text (English)
A rainbow lorikeet eats from a tree on a spring morning along the Sydney Harbour on November 2, 2024. (Photo by SAEED KHAN / AFP) SAEED KHAN/AFP via Getty Images Australia confirmed on August 23 that a long-nosed fur seal found dead near the coastal town of Beachport had tested positive for H5N1 bird flu , making it the first mammal killed by the virus on mainland Australian soil. The announcement came as researchers published an urgent warning: the 50% of Australian households that routinely feed wild birds in their backyards are now inadvertently creating disease transmission hotspots that could accelerate the outbreak into species and places the virus has not yet reached. Fur Seal Death Brings H5N1 to Australian Mainland Mammals The long-nosed fur seal was discovered on August 19 at Beachport, a coastal town approximately 310 kilometers south of Adelaide in South Australia. Testing at CSIRO's Australian Centre for Disease Preparedness confirmed the diagnosis. South Australia's Chief Veterinary Officer, Skye Fruean, explained why seals were always at elevated risk: they share rocky coastal outcrops with wild birds already carrying the virus at high concentrations. "They would be coming across, no doubt, really high levels of the virus in their environment," Fruean said in televised remarks . Federal Agriculture Minister Julie Collins confirmed the case, offering an assessment that left no ambiguity about what comes next. "Once the H5 bird flu is spreading in the natural environment it is not possible to avoid significant losses," Collins told reporters . There is, as of August 24, no evidence the seal's death is part of a broader marine mammal outbreak — but researchers and conservation advocates were quick to emphasize that Australia should anticipate one . The fur seal death is the latest step in a rapid escalation. Australia confirmed its first mainland H5N1 case on June 19, 2026 — in a migratory brown skua at Cape Le Grand National Park in Western Australia. By August 22, the federal government had confirmed 297 cases nationwide . The outbreak has already killed more than 1,000 greater crested terns in South Australia and more than two dozen little penguins in Tasmania. A silver gull confirmed H5N1-positive at Robe in early August signaled that the virus had crossed from coastal seabirds into a species capable of traveling far inland — carrying the pathogen toward agricultural zones and urban environments that have so far remained unaffected. What Your Bird Feeder Is Actually Doing Against that backdrop, Darryl Jones — Professor Emeritus at Griffith University's Centre for Planetary Health and Food Security and the author of Feeding the Birds at Your Table: A Guide for Australia — published an expert analysis on August 24 explaining specifically why backyard bird feeders are now a problem. The mechanism is not mysterious. When a feeder is stocked, it draws birds of multiple species together at a density and in combinations that would never occur naturally. An infected silver gull, a healthy rainbow lorikeet, and a scavenging magpie do not share the same perch in the wild. At a suburban feeder, cross-species mixing becomes routine . "Feeding wild birds heightens the risk of disease transmission at feeding locations. When we offer supplementary food, such as seeds or meat scraps, this attracts birds of many different species in a combination and number that would not happen in nature. They may carry deadly diseases, such as the H5N1 virus, and potentially spread them to other birds. This can create a transmission hotspot right on deck ," Jones wrote. The epidemiological logic is well-established. Deakin University epidemiologist Marcel Klaassen provided the mechanistic explanation: "Infectious diseases like influenza spread through direct and indirect contact. If you create hotspots where birds aggregate and increase contact rates — for example through bird feeders — you increase that risk," Klaassen told the Biodiversity Council . Research synthesizing more than 20 studies of supplementary wildlife feeding and infectious disease found that intentional bird feeders consistently increase bacterial and viral pathogen transmission. The mechanism is straightforward: birds become more sedentary around a food source, accumulate at higher densities than natural foraging would produce, and leave behind a contaminated surface that multiple individuals visit repeatedly across the day. How Widespread Is the Risk? Research cited by Jones has found that approximately half of Australian households feed wild birds, with many doing so daily. That figure means the behavioral change being requested — stop feeding, or at minimum change how you feed — affects roughly five to six million Australian homes. Jones's own prior research offers something the public health message needs: evidence that stopping won't hurt the birds. Even birds that visit feeders every day get less than 10% of their daily diet from those feeders, Jones's prior research found ; the rest comes from insects, seeds, and other natural sources. "Our research found that even birds visiting feeders every day typically get less than 10% of their diet there. They are still finding most of their food naturally," Jones said in his August 24 analysis . That finding changes the practical calculus: this is not a choice between keeping birds alive and stopping a disease. The birds will be fine. The feeders, on the other hand, may not be. Beyond Birds: What Gets Exposed at a Feeder-Created Hotspot The danger does not end with birds. When an infected bird dies near a feeder or in a backyard, scavenger mammals move in. Possums, red foxes, and crows that feed on carcasses of H5N1-infected birds face direct viral exposure. Given that H5N1 clade 2.3.4.4b carries a multi-basic cleavage site in its hemagglutinin protein — allowing it to be activated by enzymes present throughout the body's organs, not just the respiratory tract — mammalian infection tends to move fast and cause severe multi-organ disease. Carnivorous marsupials face a parallel risk through scavenging. Tasmanian devils and quolls, which consume the carcasses of other animals, could encounter infected bird remains in the same way the fur seal at Beachport almost certainly encountered infected seabirds. Kangaroos, wallabies, possums, and bandicoots can also scavenge meat, extending the potential exposure network further. For humans, the personal risk from the current outbreak remains low. The Australian Centre for Disease Control states that H5N1 clade 2.3.4.4b poses a low risk to people in Australia; no human infections have been detected; globally, around 100 human infections have been recorded, most involving close contact with infected poultry or dairy cattle, and most causing only mild symptoms. There has been no documented person-to-person spread of this clade anywhere in the world, according to DAFF guidance . Wildlife carers and veterinarians who handle sick or dead birds represent the highest-risk human subgroup and are advised to follow biosafety protocols strictly. The Most Endangered Are at Greatest Risk For Australia's critically endangered species, a feeder-amplified outbreak carries stakes that go beyond population loss to extinction. The orange-bellied parrot is one of Australia's most endangered birds, with just 88 individuals recorded in the wild as of the most recent survey. The species, which breeds at Melaleuca in south-west Tasmania, has suffered a dramatic loss of genetic diversity in immune genes over the past century, making it unusually vulnerable to new diseases it has never encountered before. The Tasmanian government announced plans for a vaccination program targeting orange-bellied parrots in captive breeding centers in response to H5N1's arrival in the state. For the Australian sea lion — an endangered species found nowhere else on Earth, with an estimated population of roughly 10,000 to 12,000 spread across 67 genetically isolated breeding colonies — the pathway from infected terns to sea lion deaths is now confirmed to exist, as the fur seal case makes clear. What Researchers Are Asking Australians to Do The Biodiversity Council, working with Griffith and Deakin University researchers, issued four specific recommendations for anyone who currently feeds wild birds. Stop altogether, if you can. Eliminating the food source removes the congregation point. Jones emphasized that birds do not depend on feeder food for survival: the evidence shows feeders supply a snack, not a meal. If you continue, use less food. Smaller quantities attract fewer birds, reduce crowding and cross-species mixing, and lower the contact rate that drives transmission. Clean everything daily with bleach. "With bird flu here, we need to be obsessive about hygiene. A quick wipe with a damp cloth is not enough," Jones said in his published expert guidance . Domestic bleach at standard dilution is a proven virucide for avian influenza on hard surfaces. Stop hand feeding immediately. Direct physical contact between humans and wild birds in an active H5N1 outbreak area is an unnecessary risk for both parties. Authorities are also asking anyone who spots a sick or dead bird or marine mammal to record the location, photograph the animal from a safe distance without approaching it, keep pets away, and report it to the 24-hour Emergency Animal Disease Hotline on 1800 675 888 or at birdflu.gov.au. Australia's Response Under Scrutiny The Australian government has committed more than AUD $113 million (approximately $81 million USD) to H5N1 surveillance, preparedness, and response. Of that, AUD $47.1 million (approximately $33.7 million USD) was committed before the outbreak to identify at-risk species and develop response plans, with an additional AUD $11.2 million (approximately $8 million USD) in the 2026–27 budget. A further AUD $5 million (approximately $3.6 million USD) went to 29 zoos and aquariums to protect captive threatened species , including the Tasmanian devil, orange-bellied parrot, and regent honeyeater. Conservation scientists at the Biodiversity Council — working with BirdLife Australia, the Invasive Species Council, and the Australian Marine Conservation Society — have called on the federal government to provide an additional AUD $200 million (approximately $143 million USD) over two years specifically for wildlife resilience . But the Biodiversity Council has also challenged the government to demonstrate that existing investments actually improved species resilience. Professor Hugh Possingham, the Council's Lead Councillor at the University of Queensland, publicly requested that the government release specific details on what was done with prior funding, which species benefited, and what measurable difference was made. "If Australia's wildlife is better prepared for H5 bird flu because of recent investments, we ask Government to report to Australians exactly what has been done and what difference it has made," Possingham said. Researchers identified specific response gaps : delayed risk plan releases, limited public communication, inadequate rapid-response capacity, delayed bird vaccination programs, no mammal vaccine program, and insufficient funding — particularly given Australia had years of advance notice as the virus spread through every other continent first. A vaccination program for little penguins was confirmed underway at Phillip Island, but mammalian species — including sea lions — have no equivalent protection. Millions of Australians wake each morning and scatter seed on their back decks, watching birds arrive and taking pleasure in the ritual. Scientists are not asking them to abandon that relationship permanently. They are asking them to pause it — or to change it enough that it stops functioning as a bridge between the virus and the species least equipped to survive it. Currency conversions from AUD to USD are approximate, based on the exchange rate at time of publication, and are subject to change. Frequently Asked Questions Is it safe to feed wild birds in Australia during the current bird flu outbreak? Not without precautions, and stopping entirely is the safest option. Backyard bird feeders draw multiple bird species together in concentrations that would never occur naturally, creating transmission hotspots for H5N1. Research confirms that birds get fewer than 10% of their daily calories from feeders — so stopping will not harm the birds. If you continue feeding, put out less food, stop hand feeding entirely, and disinfect feeders with bleach at least once per day. Report any sick or dead birds to the Emergency Animal Disease Hotline at 1800 675 888. Should I stop feeding birds because my backyard feeder could spread bird flu? Experts, including Professor Darryl Jones at Griffith University — who has spent decades studying Australian bird feeding practices — recommend stopping altogether as the safest course of action during the current H5N1 outbreak. The virus spreads through the kind of multi-species, high-density contact that feeders produce. If stopping feels difficult, the key steps are: dramatically reduce the amount of food offered, stop all hand feeding, and clean every surface the birds touch with domestic bleach every single day. Birds will still find the food they need in the natural environment. What species are most at risk from H5N1 in Australia right now? Australia's critically endangered orange-bellied parrot — with only 88 individuals recorded in the wild — is considered one of the highest-risk species because of its extremely low immune gene diversity. The Australian sea lion, with roughly 10,000 to 12,000 animals distributed across 67 genetically isolated colonies, faces extinction-level risk from mammal-to-mammal transmission, the pathway now confirmed possible after the Beachport fur seal death. Tasmanian devils, quolls, and other carnivorous marsupials that scavenge carcasses are also at risk. Species that congregate in large flocks — including pelicans, black swans, and brolgas — face rapid flock-wide transmission if H5N1 reaches them. Can backyard bird feeders help spread H5N1 to mammals or humans? Indirectly, yes. When an H5N1-infected bird dies near a feeder or in a suburban yard, scavenger animals — possums, foxes, crows, and others — that consume the carcass face direct exposure to the virus. This is the same pathway through which the long-nosed fur seal at Beachport likely contracted the disease, through contact with infected birds in a shared environment. Direct human infection from H5N1 in Australia remains very unlikely; the Australian Centre for Disease Control classifies the current risk as low, and no human infections have been recorded in Australia. But avoiding close contact with sick or dead wild birds is essential

Time
Event date: August 19, 2026 (fur seal discovery); Publication date: August 25, 2026. Time range: Spring 2024 to August 2026.
Location and population
Australia: Beachport, South Australia (coastal town ~310 km south of Adelaide); Cape Le Grand National Park, Western Australia; South Australia and Tasmania states; nationwide scope.
Event
First confirmed mainland Australian mammal death from H5N1 avian influenza in a long-nosed fur seal, reported by South Australia's Chief Veterinary Officer and Federal Agriculture Minister on August 23, 2026. The event is confirmed via CSIRO testing. Concurrent guidance warns that backyard bird feeders may drive H5N1 spread by increasing cross-species contact.
Scale, impact and risk
Nationwide: 297 confirmed H5N1 cases as of August 22, 2026; >1,000 greater crested terns and >24 little penguins killed; silver gull positive in early August. Risk: Feeder-created hotspots may accelerate spread to agricultural zones and urban areas; mammals (e.g., possums, foxes, Tasmanian devils, quolls, kangaroos, wallabies) face exposure via scavenging infected bird carcasses. Human risk remains low; no human infections detected in Australia.
Response, status and uncertainty
CSIRO's Australian Centre for Disease Preparedness confirmed the diagnosis. Authorities acknowledge inability to avoid significant losses once H5N1 spreads in the environment. No evidence yet of broader marine mammal outbreak. Public urged to stop or modify bird feeding; research indicates birds derive <10% of diet from feeders, mitigating harm from cessation. Wildlife carers and veterinarians advised to follow biosafety protocols. Extinction risk highlighted for critically endangered species like the orange-bellied parrot.

南澳大利亚报告海豹中第2例疑似哺乳动物禽流感病例

South Australia reports 2nd suspected mammal bird flu case in seal - chinadailyhk

B routine priorityPublic-health news

South Australia reports 2nd suspected mammal bird flu case in seal - chinadailyhk

News Brief
South Australia has reported a second suspected case of H5N1 bird flu in a long-nosed fur seal, following the first confirmed case in a mammal. The seal was found at Pages Conservation Park, a protected area off the state's south coast. The Department of Primary Industries and Regions confirmed the suspected case, with samples sent for confirmatory testing. Over 1,000 birds have been found dead in suspected H5N1 mass mortality events on three islands, including North Page and South Page. The outbreak has raised concerns about potential extinction-level events for the species. The federal government and state authorities are monitoring the situation, with the minister for the environment noting that the cases were not unexpected and more events are expected. The H5N1 strain may have originated from Heard Island, where around 13,000 elephant seal pups died from the virus in June. The outbreak highlights the growing threat of avian influenza to marine mammals and ecosystems.

从胎牛血清中分离出活 H5N1,这一发现使实验室生物安全受到新的审查 - 医学日报

Live H5N1 Was Isolated from Fetal Bovine Serum in a Finding That Puts Laboratory Biosafety Under New Scrutiny - Medical Daily
新闻简报康奈尔大学的研究人员从商业批次的胎牛血清中分离出活的传染性 H5N1 禽流感 病毒,引发了人们对实验室生物安全和生产质量控制的担忧。该血清用于全球细胞培养实验室,于 2025 年 2 月进行测试,病毒被鉴定为 高致病性禽流感 A(H5N1) clade 2.3.4.4b,基因型 B3.13。这种检测发生在从宾夕法尼亚州、堪萨斯州、内布拉斯加州、南卡罗来纳州和加利福尼亚州收集的大量奶牛中,其中受影响最大的牛群集中在加利福尼亚州。截至 2025 年 12 月中旬,19 个州超过 1,084 个牛群受到 H5N1 病毒的影响。研究发现,将血清加热至 56 摄氏度 30 分钟即可灭活病毒,这是许多商业血清产品和实验室方案中已使用的标准程序。这项研究发表在疾病预防控制中心杂志《新发传染病》上,强调需要在使用胎牛血清的实验室和生物制造设施中加强生物安全措施。这一发现不涉及巴氏灭菌乳制品、零售肉类产品或医疗 治疗,这是实验室生物安全和制造质量控制问题,而不是消费者接触故事。
查看新闻五要素
时间
该研究发表于2026年8月24日,血清批次于2025年2月进行测试。2025年12月中旬在五个州收集的血清样本中检测到了病毒。
地点与对象
胎牛血清批次是从宾夕法尼亚州、堪萨斯州、内布拉斯加州、南卡罗来纳州和加利福尼亚州的奶牛中收集的。受影响的人群包括使用这种血清的细胞培养实验室和生物制造设施。
事件
康奈尔大学的研究人员从商业批次的胎牛血清中分离出活的传染性 H5N1 禽流感 病毒,该血清用于世界各地的细胞培养实验室。该病毒被鉴定为高致病性禽流感 A(H5N1) clade 2.3.4.4b,基因型B3.13,这一发现引起了对实验室生物安全和生产质量控制的担忧。
规模、影响与风险
截至 2025 年 12 月中旬,19 个州的超过 1,084 个牛群受到 H5N1 病毒的影响,其中最集中在加利福尼亚州。血清批次中检测到 H5N1 表明使用该产品的实验室和生物制造设施存在潜在风险,但不涉及巴氏灭菌乳制品、零售肉类产品或医用 治疗。
应对、状态与不确定性
血清批次中 H5N1 的检测是通过常规外来物质检测来确定的。将血清加热至 56 摄氏度 30 分钟即可灭活病毒,这一步骤已包含在许多商业血清产品和实验室方案中。该研究是发表在CDC期刊《新发传染病》上的报告,研究结果基于实验结果。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Live H5N1 Was Isolated from Fetal Bovine Serum in a Finding That Puts Laboratory Biosafety Under New Scrutiny - Medical Daily

News Brief
Researchers at Cornell University isolated live, infectious H5N1 avian influenza virus from a commercial lot of fetal bovine serum, raising concerns about laboratory biosafety and manufacturing quality control. The serum, used in cell culture laboratories worldwide, was tested in February 2025, and the virus was identified as highly pathogenic avian influenza A(H5N1) clade 2.3.4.4b, genotype B3.13. The detection occurred in a lot collected from dairy cattle in Pennsylvania, Kansas, Nebraska, South Carolina, and California, with most affected herds concentrated in California. As of mid-December 2025, over 1,084 herds in 19 states had been impacted by H5N1. The virus was found to be inactivated by heating the serum to 56 degrees Celsius for 30 minutes, a standard procedure already used in many commercial serum products and lab protocols. The study, published in the CDC journal Emerging Infectious Diseases, highlights the need for enhanced biosafety measures in laboratories and biomanufacturing facilities using fetal bovine serum. No pasteurized dairy product, retail meat item, or medical treatment is implicated in this finding, which is a laboratory biosafety and manufacturing quality-control issue, not a consumer exposure story.

Innovation Live H5N1 Was Isolated from Fetal Bovine Serum in a Finding That Puts Laboratory Biosafety Under New Scrutiny Published Aug 24, 2026 6:30 AM EDT By Cole Mercer LUIS ROBAYO | AFP via Getty Images Share on Twitter Share on Facebook Share on Pocket Researchers at Cornell University isolated live, infectious H5N1 avian influenza virus from a commercial lot of fetal bovine serum, the growth supplement used in cell culture laboratories worldwide, in a report published in the August issue of the CDC journal Emerging Infectious Diseases. The finding matters to a specific audience, and it is worth stating plainly who that is. This is a laboratory biosafety and manufacturing quality-control story, not a consumer exposure story. No pasteurized dairy product, retail meat item, or medical treatment is implicated. The people whose practices may change are diagnosticians, cell culture technicians, biomanufacturing staff, and the institutional biosafety committees that write their protocols. The detection also carries one immediately reassuring result. Heating the serum to 56 degrees Celsius for 30 minutes, a step already built into many commercial serum products and lab protocols, inactivated the virus in the authors' experiments. Routine Screening at Cornell Caught the Contaminated Lot The lot arrived at the Virology Laboratory of the Cornell Animal Health Diagnostic Center in February 2025 for routine extraneous agents testing, a screening requirement under federal regulations governing biological products of animal origin. It was not submitted because anyone suspected contamination. Sequencing identified the virus as highly pathogenic avian influenza A(H5N1) clade 2.3.4.4b, genotype B3.13, the lineage that has been circulating in U.S. dairy cattle since March 2024. As of mid-December 2025, more than 1,084 herds in 19 states had been affected, with most concentrated in California. The tested lot was a pool of samples collected in five states: Pennsylvania, Kansas, Nebraska, South Carolina, and California. That detail is the load-bearing one for anyone assessing risk in their own supply chain, and it is addressed below. Serum Is a Pooled Product, and That Matters Fetal bovine serum is collected from bovine fetuses at slaughterhouses and is, in the paper's own framing, the most frequently used growth supplement in cell culture media. It is commonly added to basal media at concentrations of about 5-10% and supports a wide range of cell types in academic research, pharmaceutical development, and biomanufacturing. Because it is pooled from many animals, a single infected donor can contribute a large amount that is subsequently widely distributed. The Cornell team followed the positive lot with broader testing, screening individual serum samples collected at slaughterhouses in California and Texas, 384 from each state. Of those 768 samples, one tested positive by real-time reverse transcription PCR, at a cycle threshold of 35, indicating a low viral load. The authors interpret that pattern as consistent with sporadic viremia in infected lactating dairy cattle, allowing the virus to reach tissues beyond the udder, including, on rare occasions, the fetus. Follow-up testing of 13 additional bottles from the same original lot, eight of them not heat-inactivated and five heat-inactivated, is documented in a summary table accompanying the paper, using virus isolation in two cell lines, embryonated eggs, and PCR. Detection was infrequent even by PCR, and attempts to confirm virus isolation at USDA's National Veterinary Services Laboratories were not successful, which the authors take as evidence of very low viral loads in the pooled lot. Heat Treatment Cleared the Virus in Laboratory Tests The inactivation experiment is the part with an actionable conclusion. Serum aliquots were spiked with bovine H5N1 at high titer and heated. The authors report that heating at 56 degrees Celsius for 30 minutes inactivated the virus, and they conclude that although finding a zoonotic virus in serum raises biosafety concerns for diagnostic testing of bovine serum and for laboratory procedures using it, "heat treatment of FBS seems to be efficient in inactivating the virus" and could serve as a safety precaution. That temperature and duration are not novel. Commercial suppliers, including ATCC, publish 56 degrees for 30 minutes as the standard heat-inactivation protocol, and heat-inactivated serum is sold as a distinct product line. The complication is that heat inactivation is not a universal practice because suppliers caution that it is unnecessary for many cell lines and can degrade heat-labile growth factors that some cells require. The result also aligns with earlier work. Heat inactivation of H5N1 in raw cow's milk was examined in a report in the New England Journal of Medicine, part of a broader body of research into how thermal treatment affects this virus in bovine-origin material. Consumers Are Not the Audience for This Finding Several limitations go hand in hand with the headline result. This is a single documented lot, identified through routine surveillance rather than through any adverse event. No laboratory-acquired infection has been reported in connection with it. The authors note that their findings and conclusions do not necessarily reflect the official positions of the agencies involved, and that one co-author is affiliated with USDA's Animal and Plant Health Inspection Service. The paper does not establish how often serum lots are contaminated because it was not designed to. Nor does it demonstrate that anyone has been infected through this route. It documents that a live virus survived in a commercial biological product and that a widely available treatment step neutralized it under laboratory conditions. What laboratories and biomanufacturers may reasonably reconsider is whether their current serum sourcing, screening, and heat-treatment decisions account for a pathogen that was not circulating in U.S. cattle three years ago. Institutional biosafety committees, not individual bench scientists, are typically the right level for that review. Facilities working with bovine-origin material should confirm what their supplier tests for and whether their lots are heat-inactivated. The broader surveillance question remains open. Continued monitoring of H5N1 in dairy cattle and of the animal-origin products the herds feed into is tracked through the CDC journal's influenza research collection. Whether contamination of serum proves to be rare or more common than a single lot suggests will depend on testing that has not yet been reported. Read more China Reports Four More H9N2 Infections as U.S. Bird Flu Risk Stays Low for the General Public H5N1 Bird Flu Has Now Hit Over 1,166 U.S. Dairy Herds Across 19 States, With Idaho Leading the Nation More Than 100 Experts Say Ending PAHO Funding Would Weaken Early Warning for Diseases Reaching U.S. Farms Key Questions Answered What did researchers find? Live, infectious H5N1 avian influenza virus was isolated from a commercial lot of fetal bovine serum submitted to the Cornell Animal Health Diagnostic Center for routine screening. Sequencing identified genotype B3.13. Is this a risk to consumers? No consumer product is implicated. Fetal bovine serum is a laboratory and biomanufacturing reagent, not a food ingredient, and the report concerns laboratory biosafety rather than dietary exposure. Did anyone get sick from it? No laboratory-acquired infection has been reported in connection with this lot. The detection came from routine testing, not from an illness investigation. Does heat treatment solve the problem? In these experiments, heating serum to 56 degrees Celsius for 30 minutes inactivated the virus. That protocol is already standard for heat-inactivated serum products, though heat inactivation is not used for every cell line. How could a virus reach a fetal sample? The authors suggest sporadic bloodstream infection in lactating dairy cattle can allow the virus to reach tissues beyond the udder, including, in rare cases, the fetus. How common is contamination? Unknown. The study documents one positive lot and one positive individual sample among 768 samples tested at slaughterhouses in two states. It was not designed to estimate overall prevalence. Who should act on this? Institutional biosafety committees, diagnostic laboratories and biomanufacturers using bovine-origin material, who may want to confirm supplier testing and heat-treatment status for their serum lots. View five news elements
Time
The study was published on August 24, 2026, and the serum lot was tested in February 2025. The virus was detected in the serum samples collected in five states in mid-December 2025.
Location and population
The fetal bovine serum lot was collected from dairy cattle in Pennsylvania, Kansas, Nebraska, South Carolina, and California. The affected population includes cell culture laboratories and biomanufacturing facilities using this serum.
Event
Researchers at Cornell University isolated live, infectious H5N1 avian influenza virus from a commercial lot of fetal bovine serum, which is used in cell culture laboratories worldwide. The virus was identified as highly pathogenic avian influenza A(H5N1) clade 2.3.4.4b, genotype B3.13, and the finding has raised concerns about laboratory biosafety and manufacturing quality control.
Scale, impact and risk
As of mid-December 2025, more than 1,084 herds in 19 states had been affected by H5N1, with most concentrated in California. The detection of H5N1 in the serum lot indicates a potential risk to laboratories and biomanufacturing facilities using this product, but no pasteurized dairy product, retail meat item, or medical treatment is implicated.
Response, status and uncertainty
The detection of H5N1 in the serum lot was identified through routine extraneous agents testing. The virus was inactivated by heating the serum to 56 degrees Celsius for 30 minutes, a step already included in many commercial serum products and lab protocols. The study is a report published in the CDC journal Emerging Infectious Diseases, and the findings are based on experimental results.
